This role sits within Field Marketing and works cross-functionally across Marketing, DevRel, Partnerships, Sales and Design to support programs that deepen developer engagement, strengthen ecosystem relationships.

You will also support programming around Supabase Select, our flagship user conference, including partner and community programming connected to the event.

We’re a lean, remote-first team, so this role requires someone who is comfortable operating independently, experimenting with new ideas, and helping build the systems that will power Supabase’s events program as it grows.

#7112. Penn State wrestles with $124M budget hole

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The public institution projects a $87 million year-over-year drop in tuition revenue amid a fall enrollment decline.

Just over 70% of Penn State’s budget hole is due to falling tuition and fee revenue.

Behind the revenue miss is a fall headcount that is 3,058 students short of the comparable 2025 number. Breaking that down, Penn State said Monday that it enrolled 742 fewer graduate students, 922 fewer international students and 2,384 fewer out-of-state students across its campuses. All three of those groups can have an outsized impact on tuition revenue at a college.

About half of the enrollment decline came at seven regional campuses that are set to close — and had been expected. In explaining the declines, the university also pointed to nationwide trends, including widespread decreases in graduate enrollment and international students.

At the same time, Penn State logged its largest-ever undergraduate class of 43,010 students and a record class of 9,907 first-time, full-time students.

“Even with these challenges, Penn State's market position is strong,” said Matt Melvin, Penn State vice president for enrollment management, in a statement.

Health insurance costs made up the remainder of the university’s fiscal 2027 shortfall in its $2.2 billion education and general budget. By August, healthcare claims had grown 15.7% year over year, the university said Monday. Budgetwise, that led to a $37 million jump above projections.

On an FAQ page about its budget, Penn State said it had no plans for broad cuts to its programs or student services, or for changes in its hiring processes, as it looks to tighten spending. The institution also said it would hold tuition and fee prices steady despite the revenue dip.

The university does, however, already have plans to nix some 47 undergraduate programs after a multiyear review of its offerings. Collectively, those programs enroll about 1.1% of Penn State’s undergraduates. The institution listed another 50 programs as “strategic growth opportunities.”

Penn State’s plan to close seven of its 19 regional Commonwealth campuses is primarily an effort to shore up the institution’s budget. Keeping those locations open would cost the university $40 million annually in financial support and overhead expenses and require a $200 million investment in facilities, according to a 2025 report recommending the closures.

#7109. Product Builder at Camunda

Camunda is the enterprise platform for agentic orchestration, enabling organizations to coordinate AI agents, people, and systems across complex, end-to-end business processes. With built-in governance, auditability, and human oversight, Camunda gives enterprises the control they need to move AI from pilots to production — safely and at scale. Trusted by over 700 organizations worldwide, including 9 of top 10 US banks, Camunda helps enterprises boost operational efficiency, accelerate time-to-value, and deliver better customer experiences.Fully remote and global, we are in the middle of something bigger: transforming into an AI-first organisation, built on our own platform. We use Agentic AI to automate, orchestrate intelligent processes, and elevate human contribution across every team.Named GP Bullhound’s Top 100 Next Unicorn list, 2025 Great Place to Work certified. Visionary in 2025 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Business Orchestration and Automation Technologies. ranked 3rd in Flexa's 2026 Most Flexible Companies, We’re growing fast and looking for top talent to join our team. If you want meaningful work, visible impact and put something genuinely rare on your CV, keep reading.

Curious about the kind of challenges you'll work on at Camunda? Watch this quick 30-minute talk from our engineers to learn more about the new Camunda Exporter and how we’re solving complex problems at scale

We are looking for a Product Builder to join the IDP & RPA pod. You will take real problems from customers, decide what is worth solving, and build and ship the solution yourself, using AI tools to get from idea to production quickly without lowering the bar.

Product Building is how Camunda's Experience organization builds the applications people use on top of Camunda 8, such as Hub, Operate, and ProcessOS. Small pods of builders own a product area from customer problem to shipped change, with no hand-offs between product management, design, and engineering. We are setting this model up now, so you will help shape how it works.

The IDP & RPA pod builds how Camunda processes work with documents and legacy applications. With intelligent document processing (IDP), customers create extraction and classification templates in Camunda Hub and publish them so a process can use AI to turn invoices and other documents into structured data. With robotic process automation (RPA), customers integrate legacy applications that have no APIs, using Robot Framework scripts on a standalone RPA worker. The hard problems come with putting LLMs into customer processes: extraction quality, model and prompt choices, testing and trust, and working for AI agents as well as for people.

Help shape product direction for IDP & RPA by combining customer input, usage data, and field feedback into clear priorities.

Partner with the other builders and product strategy to turn problems into shipped features, using short written proposals and prototypes to align early.

Make judgment calls with incomplete information within your scope and own the outcome.

Communicate trade-offs and decisions clearly to stakeholders who don't live in the details the way you do.

Use AI tools across the whole loop, from specs to prototypes to production-ready code, and hold a high bar for what you ship by reviewing and improving the output rather than accepting it.

Build, test, and ship improvements to the IDP & RPA experience, reading errors and tracing issues far enough to give your pod useful context.

Talk to customers and to support and customer success colleagues about how IDP is used and where it breaks, and turn what you learn into decisions about what to build next.

Pick up customer incidents and trace them across Hub, the connectors, and the deployed process of the customer.

Track what you ship against real outcomes and adjust when the data tells you to.

Ability and/or willingness to use our product

#7105. Baylor stares down $30M deficit after undergraduate enrollment dip

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Baptist institution in Texas is pausing hiring and delaying capital spending as it tries to shore up the budget gap.

Livingstone’s deficit announcement follows a slide in Baylor’s undergraduate enrollment this fall.

Baylor’s total undergraduate headcount fell 2.3% year over year to 13,860 students, according to institutional data. First-time, first-year enrollment dipped 6%, representing a decline of over 200 students, which could bring ongoing financial ramifications given that they might have continued paying tuition for several years.

“Undergraduate enrollment is realigning to historic levels now that the two large COVID-era freshman classes have matriculated through the University,” Livingstone said. The university brought record numbers of students in 2020 and 2021, bucking the national trend during that period.

However, graduate enrollment increased by 3.8% to 5,888 students.

Livingstone rattled off a long list of headwinds that have dogged Baylor’s budget. Among them were rising spending, families wary of college prices, competitors increasingly discounting tuition to recruit students and “increasing volatility in forecasting budgets in an uncertain financial environment.”

Baylor’s leader also acknowledged the sum the university owes the head football coach it just fired after a slow start to the season. The buyout reportedly amounts to an estimated $12 million for Dave Aranda. Livingstone described the costs as a one-time expense not included in the deficit and “a liability of Baylor Athletics that will be addressed solely by the department.”

In the previous fiscal year, the Division I university brought in about $63 million in revenue from college sports, according to its financial statements.

Baylor started the calendar year in cost-cutting mode. In February, Livingstone announced a plan to shave $35 million from the university’s budget over two years, which she framed as necessary to limit tuition increases on students. Among other moves, the university slashed retirement contributions for employees.

In her message Monday, the university president acknowledged the sacrifices Baylor’s workforce has already made to help shore up the budget.

“As faithful Christian stewards, we must work together to prepare Baylor to succeed in a very uncertain future for colleges and universities that is likely to look quite different than the past,” she said.

Baylor joins a handful of others to issue early reports on fiscal 2027 woes amid enrollment pressures.

Just this week, Boston University leaders said the private institution missed revenue estimates by $42 million so far, and Pennsylvania State University revealed a projected $124 million budget hole due primarily to tuition revenue declines. Both institutions plan on budget cuts but did not signal that layoffs would be part of the savings efforts.

#7090. Broward schools ‘no longer the only game in town’ as enrollment drops

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Florida district is doubling down on student recruitment and retention by stepping up marketing and engagement efforts.

This is the second installment of a three-part series on the impact of private school choice on public school enrollment.

When Valerie Wanza started as a teacher in Florida's Broward County Public Schools in 1992, the district was at the beginning of what would be explosive growth in student population over the next decade.

Throughout the 1990s and early 2000s, Broward County Public Schools saw such an increase in enrollment that by 2004, more than 100 of its 315 schools were deemed "critically overcrowded."

Now, 34 years later as the district's chief strategy and innovation officer, Wanza is part of a team of administrators working to turn around an enrollment decline that began in the 2015-16 school year. The student population plummeted by 17.5% over the next decade and is forecast to continue decreasing through at least the 2030-31 school year, according to district data.

The district attributed the drop to various factors: lower birth rates leading to fewer children entering schools, rising housing costs that make it harder for families to live in the desirable area bordering the Atlantic Ocean, fewer immigrant students due to shifts in federal policies, and growing competition from private school choice options.

And with the area's school choice proliferation, Wanza said, Broward County Public Schools is "no longer the only game in town."

Now, to stay financially healthy, the district is taking a comprehensive approach that includes consolidating and closing some school campuses, cutting at least 1,000 staff positions, and implementing other measures to save money. It's also attempting to boost enrollment by vigorously recruiting new students through media campaigns, personalized phone calls, and hosting events to drum up interest in and excitement for the district's schools.

Broward is even turning to an outside vendor for help. Earlier this year, the district approved a one-year contract with student recruitment firm Caissa K12 to help connect with families who have left its public schools, determine their reasons for doing so, attempt to resolve those concerns and inform them about the district's benefits and schooling options.

The performance-based contract said Broward County Public Schools will pay Caissa K12 $850 per recruited student who enrolls in a district school, up to 176 students or a maximum payment of $150,000.

“If we aren’t real about, No. 1, recognizing what adjustments we need to make, and then be bold enough to make them, we’re going to become the Kmart … of the public schools,” Wanza said, referring to the once-dominant discount retailer that filed for bankruptcy in 2002 amid growing competition and other pressures.

The enrollment decline that Broward County Public Schools is experiencing mirrors the challenges faced by many other school districts across the country.

While falling birth rates and rising school choice are often to blame, the multitude of factors can be complex and specific to each region's ability to draw new residents with young families. Nationally, enrollment in traditional public schools is expected to drop from 54.7 million in 2021 to 49.4 million in 2035, according to a 2025 analysis from the Brookings Institution based on U.S. Census and other federal data.

On the other hand, some states are predicted to see increases in public school student populations between fall 2022 and fall 2031. Among those are Idaho (+11%), North Dakota (+7.4%) and Tennessee (+2%), according to data from the U.S. Department of Education.

Enrollment in Florida's public schools is projected to increase by 3% over the same time period.

From 2015-16 through 2025-26, Broward County Public Schools lost 17.5% of its student population, or 38,640 students. That translated to a loss of $352 million in per-pupil revenue over that 11-year span. In fiscal year 2026, which ended June 30, the district expected to show an $80 million shortfall because of continued enrollment loss.

The district has already closed seven schools since the 2025-26 school year and is exploring other closures, consolidations and grade configurations for the future. New data released this fall shows district enrollment dropped by another 10,272 for the 2026-27 school year.

Even so, with 315 schools educating 224,324 students, including at charter schools, Broward County Public Schools is still the 6th largest district in the U.S., according to its website.

Broward leaders are carefully analyzing data that can explain why families aren't enrolling their students in county schools. Joseph Beck, the district's director of demographics and enrollment planning, said his team is constantly coming up with ways to look at the data differently to better understand why students are coming or going.

For instance, the data shows that about 20,000 students, on average, withdraw from Broward County Public Schools annually. That number, which doesn't include graduating seniors, has been consistent over many years, Beck said.

The district also investigates how many new students enroll each year, a figure that can be affected by immigration policy changes. For instance, the district had 3,400 fewer newcomer students in 2025-26 than it did the previous year, Beck said.

The data also shows that 1,700 students enrolled in the 2024-25 school year transferred to a private school the following year using a Florida Family Empowerment Scholarship to help pay tuition.

At the state level, about 561,000 students participated in taxpayer-funded scholarship programs during the 2025-26 school year, according to a Florida Senate analysis. That same year, the state's public schools enrolled about 2.8 million students, state data shows.

Across Broward County, some 35,000 students participated in one of Florida's taxpayer-supported private school choice programs in the 2025-26 school year.

Beck said the data further shows that many of the county's private school choice participants weren't previously enrolled in the district's public schools. "They just completely bypass us altogether," Beck said.

He and other Broward County Public School leaders are aiming to reverse that.

Farrah Wilson, Broward County Public Schools' director of marketing and strategic communications, rapidly ticks off the efforts being made to court new students and their families. There are mailers, social media campaigns, TV ads, school tours, home visits and more.

As public schools across the country get more intentional and strategic about recruitment, they are taking a playbook from private schools. That includes hosting school tours, having "shadow days" for prospective students, and sending mailers to families encouraging them to add public schools to their list of options.

We have the best programs. We have the best teachers. We have the best facilities, athletics. We offer a comprehensive experience that many of our charter and private schools can't offer.

Broward County Public Schools' director of marketing and strategic communications

Broward County is home to 245 private schools serving about 54,000 students, or about 18% of students in the county, according to Private School Review, a site that provides information on K-12 private schools for families.

But competition for students can also come from nearby public school systems.

In a mailer that Broward sent out in mid-July, it targeted families living across the district's borders to the north in Palm Beach County and to the south in Miami-Dade County.

"This is just a way to let parents know that there are options, and if they are interested, they can go to one of our schools on the county line and participate and take one of the open seats," Wilson said.

The mailers led to one student from Palm Beach County and 17 students from Miami-Dade County enrolling in Broward County Public Schools, the district said.

While it might seem unfair to try to poach other districts' students, Broward administrators said it goes both ways. The School District of Palm Beach County has likewise attempted to recruit Broward students living near its border, Broward officials said.

The Palm Beach system shares information about its schools through various channels, and marketing reaches families across the region, including those in neighboring counties like Broward, said Sean Cooley, chief of communications and enrollment for the Palm Beach district, in an Oct. 29 email. However, Palm Beach does not track how many Broward residents enroll in its schools.

"Many families living near county boundaries commute to work within Palm Beach County, making our high-performing schools an attractive and convenient option for their children's education," Cooley said.

Florida has an open boundary policy that lets families enroll in schools outside of their home school district as long as there are open seats available. "We're used to having open lines, if you will. Now we just got more intentional about it," said Wanza.

Broward is also expanding its school choice options by catering to what families want. The district has an online school as well as 32 schools offering dual language programs. It has magnet schools, a high school that operates on a 4-day-a-week schedule, several K-8 schools, a grade 6-12 school and a K-12 school.

In another enrollment initiative, the district is encouraging and supporting each school to tell its own stories through various marketing and communications efforts. For example, on "testimonial Tuesdays," students, families and staff share on social media why their school is great.

Initiatives like this enable families to make connections with individual schools, not necessarily the district as a whole, Wilson said. Building excitement at individual schools is "the secret sauce" to boosting enrollments, she said.

Every school also has an "Enroll Now" button on its website. If a family clicks the link and shares their contact information, the school's principal is automatically notified and is encouraged to reach out directly to the family, answer any questions they have, offer a tour and make other recruiting efforts that will "close the deal," Wilson said.

One reason Broward is taking such an intentional approach to marketing, said administrators, is to combat misinformation or outdated information about district schools, including about its academic performance. Broward County Public Schools has earned an "A" rating from the Florida Department of Education for three consecutive years and continues to "demonstrate strong academic growth," the district said in an email to K-12 Dive.

Negative perceptions of the public school system are what the district is "always battling against," Wilson said. This is why the district is heavily investing in marketing and recruiting, she said.

"We have the best programs. We have the best teachers. We have the best facilities, athletics. We offer a comprehensive experience that many of our charter and private schools can't offer.”

News Graphic Developer Jasmine Ye Han contributed to this story.

#7086. Majority of students report misusing tech during school hours

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A Fordham Institute report suggests stricter bans are needed for cellphones and other personal devices, as many students still use technology to cheat.

The findings from Fordham come as the nation's largest school districts this fall announced restrictions around AI use.

In September, Los Angeles Unified School District, the nation's largest school district, announced it would restrict access to AI platforms for all students during the 2026-27 school year. The restrictions will be in place as the district reviews instructional use and safeguards for the technology, according to the district's announcement.

New York City Public Schools also announced in September it would pause AI use for elementary and middle school students.

“Though we might quibble about the details, we at Fordham agree with the impulse,” wrote Brian Fitzpatrick, national research manager at Fordham and the report’s author.

"Taken together, our results suggest that American high school students are caught up in — and often contribute to — a disturbing and interconnected set of problems: rampant distraction, widespread AI misuse, heavy dependence on screens, and grading practices that make it easier to earn high marks without mastering the material," said Fitzpatrick in the analysis.

The report was based on a nationally representative survey of over 2,000 current students and recent graduates, fielded between February and March 2026.

It found that over 50% of students use technology during the school day for social media, and just under that percentage use it to message friends. A third of students said they used it for both recreational games and YouTube viewing unrelated to assignments.

"A key reason that illicit tech use is so pervasive is that school-issued devices provide direct access to the platforms driving it," the report said. For example, two-thirds of respondents said YouTube is accessible on school-issued devices.

The report also found students were least likely to use social media when phones were fully banned, such as keeping them in a locked pouch or at the central office. Students were more likely to use social media if they were allowed access to their phones in hallways and at lunch time or if their phones were only restricted during class times.

That finding echoes another report published by the Brookings Institution in September, which found that cellphone ban enforcement tends to wane over the course of the school year as teacher enforcement wanes overtime. Bell-to-bell bans, the report found, may be more effective and durable overtime.

However, some have pushed back against all-day cellphone bans due to safety concerns for students, who cite school shootings and other emergencies as reasons to keep phones closer.

#7084. Colleges report declines in international enrollment amid federal restrictions

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Survey data released Wednesday found that nearly 6 in 10 U.S. colleges lost foreign graduate students this fall, and 45% saw a decline in undergraduates.

Wednesday’s survey, released by NAFSA: Association of International Educators in partnership with the Oxford University Press and the student recruitment platform Studyportals, reinforces concerns that the Trump administration's attacks on foreign students and the long-standing U.S. programs that support their study here would corrode the international enrollment pipeline.

Survey respondents cited the administration’s crackdown on student visas and the astronomical new fees sought for the H-1B visa and Optional Practical Training programs. While some of those policies are being challenged in the courts, they can signal to international students that they are unwelcome in the U.S., and the uncertainty and instability can ward off prospective applicants.

Major public research universities, traditionally popular among foreign students, have begun reporting international enrollment declines this fall. And some, like Pennsylvania State University, have drawn a direct line between that loss of students and budgetary challenges.

“These survey results affirm what we’ve observed before: national policies matter to international students making major decisions about where to study, conduct research, or start a career,” Fanta Aw, NAFSA’s executive director and CEO, said in a statement Wednesday.

Some colleges also reported decreased interest in countries that have historically sent the most higher education students overseas, such as China and India.

“Our agents in India in particular are telling us that they are seeing a drastic decline in inquiries for US graduate study and that many prospective students are either considering other destinations or postponing study plans,” one university respondent said.

Of those surveyed, 37% of U.S. colleges said they planned to diversify the locations where they recruit from and tap into new markets over the next 12 months.

One institution said it would pivot to the Philippines, Canada and Colombia as a means of offsetting losses from India, Bangladesh and Nepal, the three countries from which the college traditionally got the most foreign students.

Another 32% said they would expand their online offerings, and 30% said they would turn to budget cuts.

However, cuts could further exacerbate challenges for colleges’ international admissions offices. One respondent admitted that staffing issues have “resulted in a lack of attention to incoming students.”

Pollsters surveyed colleges’ senior administrators and international student recruitment professionals online between Oct. 5 and 26.

#7080. What principals can do to ease new teachers’ anxieties

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Implementing open-door policies and placing veteran educators in mentor roles are among key strategies, school leaders say.

How can principals and other school leaders give teachers a safe way to express worries or anxieties, and how does that differ for newer and more experienced teachers?

For Joe Patek, board member of the National Principals Association and principal at Nicolet Union High School in Glendale, Wisconsin, the process starts with providing “authentic spaces” to have an “honest dialogue,” recognizing that this will be awkward at first until you build trust. “You’re trying to build bridges so staff know, ‘We’ll be in the weeds with you.’”

Evelyn Edney, principal of Early College School at Delaware State University and another NPA board member, also centers relationship-building.

“People need to trust you to share [anxieties] with you,” she said, adding that it’s important for them to feel confident that you’re the person they can sit down and talk to. “When you say you have an open-door policy, you’ve got to mean that. Shut the door and [say], ‘If you need to yell, scream and cuss, let’s do it. Then let’s get to the root of the problem.’”

Building trust and promoting an open-door policy provides reassurance that you will truly listen and be solutions-minded rather than simply saying “thanks” and never meaningfully addressing the worries expressed, said Ann-Marie Gleason, president of the National Association of Elementary School Principals and principal of Harpeth Valley Elementary in Nashville, Tennessee.

It means “when folks come to you, they know that you are going to listen to them,” she said.

Once trust is established, principals can prompt teachers to openly share their stressors, both generally and regarding a specific situation or student, and then help address those issues, Patek said. Showing up in classrooms is one key to reaching that point.

“You’d be surprised how many conversations happen at the end of a five-minute [classroom] activity,” he said. “It opens the door to those authentic conversations and brings down barriers between classrooms and the principal’s office.”

Edney said she tries to alleviate stress whenever and however she can.

“I’ve had days where we have somebody practically breaking down,” she said. “I say, ‘You know what? Take the day and go home.’ They’re not going to be able to do school. Sometimes, kids do change your mood. But it’s hard when you have something gnawing at your soul.”

When concerns impact more than one teacher, Gleason suggests having a leadership team in place within grade levels and within academic departments to address those on a more comprehensive basis. That’s partly to provide different options for outlets.

“If a teacher didn’t feel comfortable initially coming straight to an administrator, they would also have that grade-level liaison to express that concern,” she said.

Newer teachers need resources to gain knowledge of local context, including from mentors while they’re onboarding and — if possible — staff who are also parents and can give a sense of how, for example, a certain politically divisive issue plays in the community, Patek said.

“What are the land mines?” he said. More experienced teachers tend to need reassurance that their skills and knowledge remain applicable as times change, he added. “While they’re seeing decades of change happening, it really is the same profession with the same mission.”

Edney said that new teacher orientation at Early College School gives way to monthly check-ins to make sure “newbies” are navigating the role well, which “takes away the angst of being in a new system,” she said.

Veteran teachers are tapped to be mentors, which helps them “feel valued and useful in school, probably even more so because of their experience,” said Edney. “They’ve been there. They’ve seen that. They’ve done that.”

At Harpeth Valley, Gleason said, new teachers and paraprofessionals are paired with longer-term staff, and specialized conversations are designed based on particular contexts and needs.

“Some best practices are the same. Some are unique to the building you’re working in,” she said. “For veteran teachers … when we make a change in curriculum or delivery or instruction or processes, it’s always important for school administrators to explain the ‘why’ behind the change. It can cause anxiety if they don’t understand the reason.”

#7078. York College nears $7.4M purchase of shuttering Penn State campus

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The private institution intends to turn the 53-acre property into a healthcare education hub through a partnership with a local hospital system.

While Penn State saw the York campus as a financial burden, York College said Thursday it sees the location as an opportunity.

York College President Thomas Burns said in a statement that the private college “is uniquely equipped to preserve this campus as a central educational hub.”

Early planning between York College and WellSpan call for an expansion of nursing and other healthcare education partnerships. Those partnerships include one struck last year between WellSpan and Temple University’s medical school to open a regional campus in York.

York College said it would finance the property acquisition with its endowment — last valued at $193.8 million — while WellSpan would commit “significant financial investment” toward capital improvements on the campus. According to Penn State, the campus had a nearly $30 million maintenance backlog as of last year.

Penn State will continue operating the campus through the spring 2027 semester as previously announced. Afterward, pending approval of the sale, York College will “lead campus operations” while WellSpan will have an “operational and instructional” presence on the campus.

Penn State opted last year to close its York campus as part of a plan to shore up its central budget and refocus on its strongest areas.

At Penn State York, enrollment declined by over 40% to 703 students between 2014 and 2024, according to a university report. Enrollment declined by 15% between 2019 and 2024.

“Despite the efforts of campus leadership and faculty to stabilize enrollment through program enhancements and community outreach, the numbers have not rebounded,” Penn State administrators wrote.

Meanwhile at York College, enrollment slid 15.1% to 3,656 students between 2019 and 2022, according to federal data. But over the next two years that increased to 3,825 students. And the college says it has seen four consecutive years of increases.

In recommending the York campus closure, Penn State administrators cited the ongoing enrollment slide, as well as “regional market saturation” and “high operating costs relative to its size and scale.”

On the last point, they pointed to challenges related to scale. Its small campus, which has 15 buildings but no student housing, made it difficult to attract students outside the commuter region.

“While its facilities are functional, the campus lacks the infrastructure or strategic location necessary to serve as a long-term hub,” the leaders wrote in their 2025 report. “As enrollment has fallen, per-student operating costs have increased, and the campus has become increasingly dependent on central resources to remain operational.”

As for regional competition, York College is just a mile away, as is a community college. Penn State’s own Harrisburg campus is 17 miles away, presenting internal competition. Altogether, there were 19 colleges within 30 miles of Penn State York, according to the university’s report.

Prior to York College’s offer, local officials had asked Penn State to donate the campus for a local arts and workforce training center.

Renata Engel, a Penn State vice president who oversees the commonwealth campuses, said in a statement Thursday, “The community planning process identified continued educational use as a priority, and this proposal would place the campus with an established local institution while also serving the University’s financial interests.”

#7076. Attorney, New Verticals at Atticus

At any given time, 16 million Americans are experiencing a crisis that requires urgent help from our legal system or government. The right assistance could transform their lives. But today, most never get it.

Atticus makes it easy for any sick or injured person in crisis to get the life-changing aid they deserve. In the last six years, we've become the leading platform connecting people with disabilities to government benefits. We’re also quickly scaling an injury practice to help victims of accidents, misconduct, and violence get compensation from insurance.

So far, we've helped hundreds of thousands of people access over $10B in life-changing aid — and earned over 20,000+ five-star reviews. And we're just getting started.

We've raised more than $100 million from top VC firms like Forerunner, GV (Google Ventures), and True Ventures, with ambitions to create a category defining business assisting Americans in need.

Each month, tens of thousands of Americans seek our help for a single legal matter – applying for Social Security Disability, filing a Workers’ Compensation claim – but many of these clients are actually eligible for additional aid without ever knowing it.

Your job as Lead Attorney, New Verticals is to help us do right by these clients, helping them claim additional benefits through the social safety net. You’ll oversee the team of intake specialists who speak with clients who have secondary claims, evaluating the case facts and whether to connect them with an Atticus attorney or local attorney to provide legal advice and potentially take on their case.

This is a job that straddles the line between practicing the law and growing a business. It requires developing a strong understanding of multiple fields of law, and being able to accurately identify when we can help and when we cannot. But even more importantly, this job requires you to be an incredibly strong operator: you need to keep the big picture in mind and stay laser-focused on how we can achieve our goals, oftentimes finding outside-the-box ways to accomplish them. You will report to our General Manager of New Verticals and work closely with our CEO and Managing Attorney.

On any given day, you’ll spend your time:

Speaking with severely injured clients trying to rebuild their lives after a catastrophic accident

#7073. Rigorous course participation rose after 2021 law, University of Illinois report finds

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Increases in dual credit and Advanced Placement participation in Illinois were particularly notable for Black students and students with disabilities.

Previous research shows links between success in programs like dual credit and AP, and greater college access and persistence.

Statewide, participation in dual credit courses rose by 17.1% overall in 2023-24 and another 9.1% in 2024-25, as compared with 1.6% and 6.9% in the two previous school years, respectively. Statewide participation in AP courses rose 6.6% in both 2023-24 and 2024-25, as compared with 2.3% the previous year and a participation drop of 5.9% in 2021-22.

While these and other gains were made in the immediate aftermath of the legislation, the study did not attempt to prove a causal relationship, said Sarah Cashdollar, associate director of the Illinois Workforce and Education Research Collaborative.

“Many other things are happening in the state that could be responsible,” she said, including amendments to the state’s Dual Credit Quality Act and higher state appropriations for dual credit and AP courses. “The state, the legislature, and all those who support it are throwing a lot at this issue in trying to improve participation in programs,” she said. “It’s hard for researchers to disentangle the effects of each of them separately.”

Nonetheless, the data describes a positive trend, said Meg Bates, IWERC director. “Certainly, it would have been a disappointment to see it go in the other direction,” she said. We’re excited to see it, and we’ll keep watching it. Hopefully we can do that kind of causal study in the future.”

The IWERC study bolsters other research that shows enrollment in rigorous courses is critical for underrepresented populations, Bates said.

For dual credit, the study found participation rates in 2023-24 increased from the previous year by 28.4% for Latino students and 25.6% for Black students. The year-over-year participation rate increase for Asian students was 15.6% and 13.9% for White students.

For AP, the increases were 9.2% for Latino students, 8.5% for Black students, 4.6% for White students and 3.4% for Asian students.

“Sometimes, we see a general trend that looks one way, but when you go underneath the hood, changes in certain students’ participation” runs counter to the trend, she said. “In this case, we got to see a little bit of both.”

However, she added, “That’s great, seeing more Black and Latino students, and more English learners. But because it’s going up for everyone, some of our gaps are widening.”

Those gaps should spur further research, Bates said, because educators and policymakers don’t want to see student experiences become more asymmetrical. “Following up with districts to understand the ‘why’ is very important.”

Cashdollar would like to see more research into the spikes in participation for English learners and Latino students.

“Was this group previously overlooked despite being capable of taking these classes — so the policy did what it was intended to do, and qualify more students?” she said. “A better understanding of all the different factors at play would be really useful.”

Bates believes a policy like automatic enrollment is uniquely equipped to target English learners. “Educating English learners can be costly for districts and can be hard to staff because you need bilingual educators,” she said.

This is a "nudge" to include English learners in this coursework," Cashdollar said.

"That’s a huge takeaway and something advocates are very excited about.”

#7072. Trump taps DEI opponent to lead DOJ’s civil rights division

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

While at the U.S. Department of Education, Craig Trainor threatened to strip federal funding from colleges over their diversity initiatives.

Trainor’s selection signals that the Trump administration isn’t backing away from its heavy crackdown on colleges and their diversity initiatives. Trainor is set to replace Harmeet Dhillon, one of several top DOJ officials who stepped down after Democrats seized control of both chambers of Congress in the midterm elections.

Under Dhillon, the department’s civil rights division aggressively investigated colleges over pro-Palestinian protests and their policies for admissions, leading several high-profile colleges to cut deals with the Trump administration to end those probes.

The DOJ has even sued the University of California system, alleging that its Los Angeles campus hasn’t adequately responded to harassment toward Jewish and Israeli students.

The agency also sued Harvard University in February to force the Ivy League institution to hand over extensive admissions data and again in March to cut off its access to federal student aid. Judges dismissed both cases against Harvard, but the agency has since appealed the federal student aid case to the 1st U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals.

DOJ officials have recently stepped up their crusade against initiatives aimed at recruiting a diverse study body. So far, its investigations into admissions policies have led the department to accuse 10 medical and law schools of racially discriminating against White and Asian applicants.

Trainor has likewise spearheaded major efforts to stamp out DEI in the higher education sector. While serving as the Education Department’s acting assistant secretary for civil rights, Trainor threatened in February 2025 guidance to pull federal funding from colleges over DEI initiatives ranging from race-based scholarships to affinity graduation ceremonies.

Federal courts have since struck down the guidance. However, the DOJ released similar sweeping guidance last year that threatens to strip federal grant funding from colleges over what the agency deems as illegal DEI. DOJ officials flagged several commonplace practices as potentially unlawful, ranging from requiring job applicants to provide diversity statements to having identity-based lounges — even if they are open to all.

Trainor pursued similar anti-DEI policies in his most recent appointment as the U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development’s assistant secretary for fair housing and equal opportunity. In that role, Trainor issued guidance warning colleges that affinity housing may violate federal law.

#7067. Engineering Manager - Pipelines at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We're looking for an Engineering Manager to lead the Pipelines team at Supabase. Supabase Pipelines is a managed change data capture product. It copies the tables you select from Supabase Postgres to other systems, then streams later changes. The product is in public alpha, with BigQuery, ClickHouse, DuckLake and Snowflake destinations.

Pipelines uses Postgres logical replication and Supabase ETL, our open source replication engine written in Rust. The team works close to the database. That means replication slots, WAL retention, initial table syncs, and the load they place on a customer's source database. The team also builds and runs the destination connectors. Correctness, ordering and recovery matter there as much as speed.

#7066. Normalizing ‘productive struggle’ could curb science anxiety

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Students can become too focused on finding “one right answer” when science is ultimately about experimentation and exploration, one educator says.

Whether they’re facing biology, chemistry or physics classes, not all students feel assured they’re a “science person” and can build enough competence and understanding to succeed, according to a former president of the National Science Teachers Association.

“I do think some students likely experience ‘science anxiety’ for a variety of reasons,” said Christine Royce, who’s also an education professor at Shippensburg University and co-director of the master’s in STEM education program.

One proximate cause is the high-stakes assessment system in the U.S., which instills a belief that there’s always one right answer to any given question, Royce said.

“That becomes problematic in science even more so, because while there are ‘more’ correct answers, science is also about exploration and experimentation,” she said. “They’re looking for, ‘What is the answer I’m supposed to give you?’ The problem is, depending on what data you’ve collected, that’s open to interpretation.”

But that open-endedness is often counterintuitive to how students have been prepared to face the “assessment era,” which can produce cognitive dissonance and result in anxiety, Royce said.

“If you’re looking at strategies to try to reduce science anxiety, one of the best things teachers can do is start to normalize the idea that productive struggle is part of the process,” she said. “They can learn from mistakes and move forward. That’s one of the best ways teachers can support students in science education.”

To strengthen that link, science teachers should help students recognize how what they’re learning about in the classroom is connected to the real world, Royce said.

“If they have a more grounded understanding of what’s going on because they can see it as part of their environment, they can see it as concrete, they will have a better ability to work through the connections,” she said. “If there’s no connection, students are, perhaps, guessing at answers."

On the other hand, "if there’s a connection, they’re using the information they already know to make predictions and draw conclusions,” Royce said.

Another method Royce recommends for helping students better understand science and reduce their anxiety around the topic is to encourage working with peers or in groups, to develop a wider notion of potential discoveries and answers they could gin up.

The dialogue between peers can help students make sense of what they're learning, she said. “That also helps them to realize that it’s OK to struggle. And while they may identify as not being ‘science people,’ science is part of our world, and it’s important for them to recognize that with science knowledge, they’re going to be better-informed about any field they go into.”

#7063. New Industry Alliance to Work Toward Common Security Model for AI Agents

Okta has announced a coalition bringing together major cloud, security, data and application vendors to create a shared architecture for securing AI agents across enterprise environments.

The 12 founding members of the new Blueprint Alliance are AWS, CrowdStrike, Databricks, Docker, Google Cloud, Lovable, Okta, Proofpoint, Salesforce, ServiceNow, Wiz and Zscaler. GE Appliances and World Central Kitchen are participating as strategic advisors.

The effort expands an earlier Okta framework into an open, multi-vendor reference architecture intended to address a basic problem with enterprise AI agents: An agent can be created in one system, authenticated through another, access tools and data from several others, and be monitored by yet another security platform.

The Alliance is focused less on how agents are built and more on how organizations can govern them consistently across vendor boundaries.

The Blueprint Alliance architecture organizes agent security around four questions: Where are my agents? What can they do? What are they doing? How do I respond?

Those questions translate into several practical areas:

The reference architecture also covers development-related systems. Its whitepaper includes agent frameworks, code repositories, CI/CD environments, code assistants, tool registries, Model Context Protocol servers, and command-line tools among the components that may need to be discovered or governed.

That makes the initiative relevant to development teams deploying agents that interact with internal APIs, MCP tools, databases, SaaS applications, or other agents. The architecture calls for those connections and permissions to remain visible and controllable as agents move from development into production.

A major part of the Alliance's work will be testing how security information and controls can move between products from different vendors.

The initial architecture references several existing standards and protocols, including MCP for agent-to-tool interactions, the Open Cybersecurity Schema Framework for security telemetry, and the Shared Signals Framework and Continuous Access Evaluation Profile for exchanging risk information.

The goal is for one part of an enterprise stack to be able to detect a risk condition and pass that information to another control point that can act on it. Depending on the implementation, that could include revoking access or containing an agent whose behavior has changed.

The Alliance says members will build and test reference integrations and publish results as interoperability work progresses. Specific cross-vendor implementations and performance results were not documented in the launch announcement.

Founding member Lovable also published a developer-oriented explanation of its participation, describing organizations that may have internally built agents, agents imported through SaaS platforms and locally running agents that all need to be inventoried and governed under a common model.

The Alliance's whitepaper recommends that organizations begin with foundational capabilities such as unified logging, telemetry, and agent discovery rather than attempting to deploy every control at once.

The architecture is available for community use, and the Alliance says it will continue evolving as members test integrations and additional participants become involved. Timelines for individual vendor integrations have not been published.

For more information, check out the Okta announcement here.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

Governance of the Open Secure AI Alliance has moved to the Linux Foundation, giving the initiative what the organizations describe as a neutral home for developing open source tools, shared standards and defensive practices. The Alliance was launched by NVIDIA in July to develop open security technologies for AI systems and agents.

Cybersecurity companies Rubrik and CrowdStrike have announced an expanded identity-security integration designed to move compromised identity environments from threat detection through clean recovery with less manual intervention.

CoSN, the professional association for K-12 education technology leaders, has released new guidance to help schools navigate the screen time debate and develop technology policies that protect students while supporting learning.

#7061. Engineering Manager - Realtime at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We're looking for an Engineering Manager to lead the Realtime team at Supabase. Realtime lets developers send low-latency messages between clients, track and synchronize user state, and listen to database changes. Those three features are Broadcast, Presence and Postgres Changes. Broadcast also sends from the database and over HTTP, and clients can replay recent messages.

Realtime is a globally distributed Elixir cluster built on Phoenix. The team balances low latency, fair limits between projects, and the cost of running the service. Customers expect connections to stay up, so reliability matters as much as new features.

The Engineering Manager is a people, delivery and technical role. We don't expect you to write production code, but you you will lead a team of experienced engineers. We do expect you to join design discussions and help set the bar for engineering quality, testing and operations.

Build a healthy, high-trust team culture in a remote-first, globally distributed environment.

Run 1:1s, performance management, career development, and onboarding for your direct reports.

Handle people admin (leave, time off, etc.) for your team.

Own the hiring plan for your team and act as Hiring Manager. First round of interviewer for all roles on the team.

Partner with Talent Acquisition to source, interview, and close strong candidates.

Contribute to headcount planning, org design, and the evolution of engineering leadership at Supabase.

Own the team's roadmap and project management (Linear), driving accountability and clear milestones.

Manage workflow intake and Slack triage. These are the team’s primary inboxes for cross-team requests. You decide what gets routed where, prioritized, or pushed back on.

Own the team's weekly updates and keep stakeholders aligned without creating overhead.

Organize effort against support tickets and maintain the team's SLO.

Surface risks, dependencies, and tradeoffs early and clearly to leadership.

Train as an Incident Commander. Drive coordination, resolution, and post-incident follow-up.

Have 5+ years of engineering background, with enough depth to be credible with engineers building complex systems.

Have 3+ years managing remote engineering teams.

Have managed a team that built and ran a low-latency, high-connection service. Examples are WebSockets, pub/sub, messaging, presence or streaming systems.

Have strong technical credibility in distributed systems. We look for clustering, rate limiting, backpressure and capacity planning.

Have led a team through production incidents on a customer-facing service. You ran the follow-up work and kept the review blameless.

Understand what developers need from a real-time API or SDK, and have shipped one to customers outside your company.

Have experience with Elixir, Erlang or BEAM (not required but a plus).

Have experience with Postgres logical replication and Row Level Security (not required but a plus).

Have managed remote, globally distributed teams that work asynchronously across disparate time zones.

Are technical enough to be credible with strong engineers, but genuinely passionate about people management. This is the work you want to do, not a track you're settling for.

Build psychological safety and a feedback culture. You give feedback often and in the moment, ask for it back, and create space for the team to do the same.

Operate as a multiplier. You help individuals achieve more than they could alone, and you don't need to be the best technical voice in the room.

Coach rather than direct. You remove blockers, connect people to context, and trust your engineers to own how the work gets done.

Have a real track record of performance management and can speak to specific examples of difficult conversations.

Are comfortable with ambiguity and can build structure where there isn't much.

Communicate clearly and write well. You default to async and written, set context early, and surface risks early.

Are collaborative, low-ego and adaptable. You thrive with high-autonomy senior engineers who don't need you to be the smartest person in the room.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

#7060. COVID learning recovery falls on states’ shoulders, CRPE report says

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Only about 1 in 10 students are in districts that are back to prepandemic achievement levels in math and reading, per the Center on Reinventing Public Education.

Pandemic-related learning recovery has "failed" for most students, and now states need to take the lead in accelerating academic achievement and driving improved student outcomes, according to a new report from the Center on Reinvening Public Education.

Only 10.5% of students are in districts that have returned to prepandemic achievement levels in both math and literacy. No states have returned to prepandemic attendance levels, according to the report. And with a downsized U.S. Department of Education shifting more responsibilities to the states, "all eyes [are] on states at a very pivotal moment," said Robin Lake, director of CRPE and co-author of the report. Lake was speaking at a Nov. 9 webinar on the report's findings, along with co-author Chelsea Waite, a research principal at CRPE. The report was released last month jointly with Arizona State University's Mary Lou Fulton Teachers College, where CRPE is housed.

To improve student outcomes, state leaders should investigate gaps between their policies and how they are implemented — including systems for measuring their progress and accountability performance. Then, they could prioritize plugging those gaps by paying special attention to the students furthest behind, including those with disabilities and multilingual learners, the report said. While academic performances varies widely among states, CRPE's analysis shows that every state has room for improvement. The analysis found that only 13 states have clear, specific, statewide goals for student learning that is messaged across multiple documents and webpages, said Waite.

South Carolina, for instance, has set a goal for 75% of its students to perform at grade level by 2030. "A clear goal is the first piece of a clear strategy," Waite said. "So without a clear goal, there's no way to tell whether a state is doing something that adds up to anything, and nobody can hold the state to that goal if it's not made public."

The CRPE report explores how state education policies, initiatives and reforms break down along political lines. The center found partisan divides in state-led instructional reform for math and literacy. For example, eight of the 12 states with “strong” policies for science of reading teacher preparation programs were categorized as red states, and 13 of 18 states with math laws were Republican-led.

On the other hand, student well-being policies — such as initiatives that touch on counseling, suicide prevention and school safety — are "led almost entirely by Democrats," the report said. Of the 17 states that have policies giving mental health days that can count as an excused absence from school, three are red states, eight are blue and six are purple.

The report found that states' academic achievement spanned the political spectrum, even though a "popular narrative" is that blue states outperform the others.

As far as how midterm elections will influence state education policy, that's a "big unknown," said Waite. The analysis was conducted before the November elections.

The report examines state policy adoption and implementation in four core areas:

Waite said the CRPE report is not claiming a certain combination of reforms will drive improved student outcomes. But, she said, early evidence suggests that "taking on several interconnected reforms at once does really seem to matter.”

#7056. Proposed SNAP rule would threaten nearly 2M children’s school meal access

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A proposed rule by the USDA would mean those children would no longer be categorically eligible for free school meals, despite 97% of them still qualifying.

The changes would come as a result of the USDA eliminating “broad-based categorical eligibility,” which is a state option that streamlines SNAP applications for households that already qualify for other assistance programs. More than 40 states and the District of Columbia use this option.

Eliminating this eligibility "may result in some level of increased administrative burden on states, a reduction in SNAP benefit spending, and associated effects on school meals eligibility," the USDA acknowledged in its proposal.

In addition to impacting school meal eligibility, the department estimates that approximately 42,000 children could potentially no longer receive the Summer Electronic Benefit Transfer beginning in summer 2029. The SEBT allows $120 in grocery benefits per eligible school-age child when school is out for summer.

The USDA’s new proposed rule would further exacerbate challenges for school meals, as well as students' benefits for summer meals, according to the Food Research & Action Center.

"Eliminating BBCE would reduce access to free school meals, making children hungry at home and at school," said FRAC in a Nov. 10 statement. "Children who lose SNAP are no longer automatically eligible for free school meals."

It would also increase administrative work for schools, such as those who were previously eligible for Community Eligibility Provision, the organization said. CEP helps high-poverty schools provide universal access to free school meals. The organization said it is concerned the impact could be “far worse than projected."

The public comment period on the proposed rule closes Dec. 10. The rule has so far received over 200 comments as of Thursday.

School districts have faced a slew of budget and eligibility challenges related to school meal programs under the second Trump administration.

In fall 2025, a prolonged government shutdown caused school systems and government agencies to provide outreach and extra supports for low-income families and children affected by the likely expiration of benefits.

Earlier that year, the Republican-led One Big, Beautiful Bill Act that went into effect July 2025 was estimated to reduce SNAP participation by roughly 2.4 million people in an average month between 2025-2034, putting school meals for children from those households at risk as they lost direct certification for free school meals.

The OBBBA changes also impacted CEP for districts. In Illinois, for instance, 47 fewer schools and 15 fewer districts participated in CEP between the 2024-25 and 2025-26 school years.

In addition to a reduction in eligibility, school district leaders have been concerned about the impact of federal budget cuts on school meals.

In May, 74% of school districts were concerned about the effect on school meals from looming federal budget cuts to SNAP and Medicaid, according to a report released by FRAC.

And in a September report by AASA, The School Superintendents Association, a majority — 67% — of superintendents said current funding levels are moderately or significantly hindering school meal programs. Two in 5 leaders said they had to pull funding from elsewhere in their district budgets to cover the cost of feeding students last year, including tapping into their rainy day balances or deferring maintenance upgrades.

When free school meals are no longer available to all students, teachers report an increase in student hunger and behavior issues negatively impacting students' focus on learning, according to a study published in September in the Journal of Nutrition Education and Behavior. The study reviewed the impact of the end of nationwide, pandemic-era universal school meals in June 2022.

#7053. AI Platform Behind 12,000 E-mail Breaches Dismantled

The platform moved quickly. Since launching in February, EvilTokens had been tied to more than 12,000 compromised inboxes across over 10,000 organizations worldwide, according to Microsoft. Its victims included businesses and institutions in financial services, healthcare, construction, real estate, wholesale distribution, and higher education.

Microsoft and its partners seized 50 websites used to operate EvilTokens and disabled more than 150 additional domains supporting the service. The Metropolitan Police Service in the United Kingdom also arrested two men on Sept. 11 in connection with the alleged operation.

At the center of EvilTokens was an AI chatbot designed specifically for cybercrime. Once attackers gained access to an inbox, the chatbot could analyze messages, map relationships, identify employees involved in payments, and recommend ways to impersonate trusted contacts.

Microsoft said the platform helped criminals determine "who to target, who to impersonate," and how to get the most money from a compromised organization.

Business e-mail compromise has traditionally required attackers to manually comb through messages, learn how an organization handles payments, and find the right person to impersonate. However, EvilTokens simplified the entire process by automating much of that work.

The tools could quickly sort through a stolen inbox, summarize or translate messages, find invoices and wire-transfer discussions, and identify the people who moved money. EvilTokens could then draft messages that sounded like they came from the victim. It packaged all of this with mailbox access, phishing tools, management dashboards, and even customer support.

The service was sold through Telegram for a $1,500 startup fee, followed by a $500 monthly subscription. Microsoft's complaint also described a bulk e-mail tool that cost $600 a month and a more advanced e-mail delivery platform priced at $1,000 a month.

To break into a target, EvilTokens relied on device-code phishing. Victims were sent to a real Microsoft sign-in page and persuaded to enter an authentication code. The login process looked legitimate because it was, but the code quietly authorized a session controlled by the attacker.

That also made cleanup more complicated. The attackers stole OAuth tokens and authenticated sessions instead of passwords, so a password reset alone might not remove their access. Administrators also needed to revoke the stolen tokens and active sessions.

According to Microsoft's complaint,  EvilTokens used fake Microsoft 365 security notices and other branded templates to direct users into the device authentication flow. Once inside, attackers could use Microsoft Graph and other APIs to search and monitor a mailbox.

The company also found that AI played a role in creating EvilTokens itself. Investigators said much of the platform appeared to have been "vibe coded," with its developers using AI to quickly build and expand the service. EvilTokens reportedly drew on multiple AI models, including improperly integrated services from Groq and OpenAI.

The court authorized Microsoft to take control of or disable domains connected to the operation. Microsoft worked with Cloudflare, Coinbase, OpenAI, Railway, SpyCloud, the Shadowserver Foundation, and TRM Labs on the disruption. Health-ISAC joined the case because healthcare organizations were among the targets.

This marks the Microsoft Digital Crimes Unit's 40th court-authorized disruption and its first targeting an end-to-end, AI-enabled cybercrime service.

EvilTokens' infrastructure may be offline, but the model behind it is unlikely to disappear. The operation showed how AI can turn a stolen inbox into an immediately useful fraud playbook, allowing less-experienced criminals to launch attacks that once required considerable time and skill.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#7049. Redesigned Microsoft Copilot Will Build Apps and Work on Its Own

Micorosft has announced that it's redesigning Copilot, the company's all-encompassing AI assistant, so users can hand off complex tasks, build tools by describing what they need, and assign work to an agent that keeps going after they log off.

The new Copilot experience has three main parts: Home, Code, and Autopilot. It also brings Word, Excel, and PowerPoint into the Copilot app. For users, the pitch is simple: Start a task in one place and stay there as it grows from a quick question into a document, an app, or a longer-running project. Most of the new capabilities are still rolling out or in preview.

Home puts Chat and Copilot Cowork together. Chat remains the place for quick answers, research, and drafts. Cowork handles work a user wants to delegate, such as preparing a briefing or pulling together a response to a request for proposals. Microsoft said Copilot eventually will route requests to Chat, Cowork, or Code on its own.

Office in Copilot will allow users to create a Word document, Excel workbook, or PowerPoint deck without leaving that experience. The files remain editable in the regular Office apps, and colleagues can work on them at the same time. Someone building a presentation, for example, could draw on a workbook, draft the slides, and keep refining both files from within Copilot.

Code is aimed at people who need a tool but might never open a development environment. A user can describe a tracker, dashboard, app, or workflow in plain language and have Copilot build it. Microsoft calls these "small, purpose-built solutions." The feature uses the same underlying technology as GitHub Copilot and runs in a sandboxed environment. A separate Copilot Managed Runtime, now in preview, lets organizations host and share the resulting apps within Microsoft 365.

Then there is Autopilot, previously known as Scout. It is designed for work that does not end with a single response: following a discussion, checking in with stakeholders, or keeping a project moving over several days. "Give it a name, a role, and a goal," Microsoft said. Autopilot runs in the cloud and has its own identity, memory, and workspace in the customer's tenant. Microsoft said its actions are subject to permissions, auditing, and organizational controls.

Those longer-running tasks also change how customers pay for Copilot. In a separate pricing post, Microsoft said everyday work in Chat and Microsoft 365 apps will remain covered by a per-user subscription. Cowork, Code, and Autopilot will be billed separately through usage-based Copilot Credits. A Copilot user subscription will still be needed to access those features.

For enterprise customers, the features that charge by usage will stay off until an admin sets up a spending policy in the Microsoft 365 admin center. Microsoft said there will be no charges before then. Admins can set budgets for the whole organization or for specific groups, limit how much individual users can spend, and get alerts when costs approach a set amount. Reports will show who is using the features and which tasks are driving costs.

Microsoft is bringing Code and Copilot Managed Runtime under those cost controls alongside Cowork. Copilot Studio agents are expected to follow in October. Before turning the features on for more employees, IT departments will need to decide who can use them, how much they can spend and which tasks are worth the extra cost.

Home and Code will begin rolling out through Microsoft's Frontier early access program in the coming weeks. Code's Frontier rollout starts at the end of September, when Autopilot also expands to private preview.

For more information, visit the Microsoft site.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.
