#6965. Researchers Find AI Is Accelerating Vulnerability Discovery

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

In a recent blog post, the company stated that in September 2026, vendors addressed 998 common vulnerabilities and exposures (CVEs) around Patch Tuesday, compared with 96 during the same period last year. In addition, the three-month rolling average of patched Linux kernel vulnerabilities tripled year over year, while Google's Chrome browser experienced a 36-fold increase.

The researchers point to advanced AI models, including Anthropic's Claude Mythos Preview, as a major factor behind the increase in vulnerability discovery.

Introduced in April 2026, Mythos Preview is being used through Project Glasswing, an initiative involving Microsoft, the Linux Foundation and other organizations to identify vulnerabilities in critical software.

While AI could ultimately make software more secure, the cybersecurity company warns that vulnerability discovery is accelerating faster than organizations can deploy fixes.

IT teams must still evaluate patches, test compatibility and schedule updates without disrupting critical business operations.

The challenge is particularly significant for organizations already struggling with patch management.

The cybersecurity vendor cites Verizon's 2026 Data Breach Investigations Report, which found that 35% of known actively exploited vulnerabilities remained unpatched after 28 days in 2025.

Ryan Powell, vice president of IT and security at ThreatDown, said automated patching systems can help organizations manage the growing workload. However, additional patches still require more testing and validation, increasing operational overhead and the risks associated with frequent system changes.

The report also highlights the risks of vulnerabilities in widely used software components such as OpenSSL, embedded in applications, network devices, and security products.

Unlike standalone applications, these components often depend on downstream vendors to release updates before organizations can deploy fixes.

The security firm's analysis points to CVE-2022-0778, an OpenSSL vulnerability patched in March 2022, as an example. Some affected products did not receive updates until several months later.

As AI accelerates vulnerability discovery, these dependencies could delay remediation and extend organizations' exposure to known security flaws.

ThreatDown's findings illustrate a growing challenge for enterprise cybersecurity: AI is making vulnerabilities easier to discover but fixing them remains a complex and time-consuming process.

For the full report, visit the ThreatDown blog here.

#6961. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

#6954. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

#6953. Time to Break Down That Major Twist at the End of ‘Campus Drivers’ and How It Differs From the Books

The Prime Video series takes a major turn from C.S. Quill’s series.

If—after watching the sixth episode of Campus Drivers—you wondered whether there were still more episodes to come, I’ve got bad news: That really was the finale. Season 1 of the French series from Prime Video ends with a twist that leaves plenty of questions, especially about Lane and Lois’s future. It’s a choice that even surprised those familiar with the book series by C.S. Quill, because the TV ending takes a different direction from the novel’s.

But what exactly happens in the final episode? And, above all, why does Lane decide to leave for Los Angeles right when his relationship with Lois finally seemed destined to take off? Let’s get into it.

In the season 1 finale, we discover that Lane leaves with his mother to move to L.A. and attend film school—a decision that, at first glance, seems to come out of nowhere, especially considering the bond that has formed with Lois over the course of the episodes. Behind this choice, however, there is a secret motivation: Lane’s mother does not approve of the path her son wants to take and believes his career choice is not the right one, but she accepts it on the condition that he leave Bellerose and end his relationship with Lois; otherwise, she is prepared to interfere with Lois’s scholarship, since she considers her an unsuitable partner for Lane.

At that point, Lane is faced with an impossible decision: stay beside the person he is falling in love with, or sacrifice his own happiness to allow her to continue studying medicine at a prestigious university.

Lane chooses the second option. He agrees to leave with his mother, thereby giving up the healthiest relationship of his life just when the two of them were finally beginning to truly grow closer.

The season’s final scene makes the separation even more concrete. In fact, we see movers carrying the furniture out of Lane’s apartment: an image that confirms his departure and the fact that the move to the United States is not just a threat or a possibility, but a decision that has now been made.

What’s more, Lois does not know the real reason for Lane’s departure. She does not know that he agreed to leave only to protect her future and allow her to continue her studies. In her eyes, that separation therefore risks seeming like a personal choice, a renunciation of their story just when it could have begun. At this point, the series departs considerably from the novel: the TV ending changes the protagonists’ journey and chooses to interrupt their story at a particularly delicate point, leaving the most important issue unresolved: the two protagonists did not get their romantic happy ending.

The ending seems designed precisely to leave the door open to a possible sequel (with the two of them still as major protagonists) and to heighten the drama—although for now, an official renewal has yet to be announced. Here’s hoping we will see Lane and Lois again in a second season!

#6949. Here’s How Much the Mirrorball Trophy Is Worth on ‘Dancing With the Stars’

We’re just a few weeks out from the Dancing With the Stars season 35 finale (it goes down on November 24), which means one couple is about to walk away with the Len Goodman Mirrorball Trophy. You know, the giant disco ball that everyone hoists above their heads after winning?

And if you’re wondering how much it’s worth, the answer is PRICELESS, obviously. But also, kind of a lot.

The mirrorball itself is incredibly cheap. Production designer James Yarnell told People in 2017, “No matter how much we put into the trophy, how much we spend, it always has a $10 mirrorball on top.” The show does throw down for the base, however. Winner Kaitlyn Bristowe told Trading Secrets that, all together, the trophy is worth around $10,000.

Apparently Hollywood 3D Printing—who uses “3D printing technologies combined with advanced finishing capabilities such as detailed painting, electroplating, and vacuum plating”—is responsible for making the Mirrorball Trophies, which they confirmed on Instagram in 2023.

Meanwhile, this glitzy statue has come a long way since the early days of Dancing With the Stars. Yarnell said he made the original Mirrorball Trophy (which Kelly Monaco won) from a brass lamp, a wooden base, and some tape. And it stayed that way for four full seasons! Per Yarnell, “They stopped making the lamp at Lamps Plus, so we’d run out of options and had to start casting it and put a heavy acrylic base with a brass stem.”

As for whether or not celebs and pros keep their trophies, it presumably depends on the person. But Derek Hough is so attached to his collection that he literally travels with them on tour and puts them in the lobby for fans to enjoy.

#6947. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

Governance of the Open Secure AI Alliance has moved to the Linux Foundation, giving the initiative what the organizations describe as a neutral home for developing open source tools, shared standards and defensive practices. The Alliance was launched by NVIDIA in July to develop open security technologies for AI systems and agents.

Cybersecurity companies Rubrik and CrowdStrike have announced an expanded identity-security integration designed to move compromised identity environments from threat detection through clean recovery with less manual intervention.

CoSN, the professional association for K-12 education technology leaders, has released new guidance to help schools navigate the screen time debate and develop technology policies that protect students while supporting learning.

#6944. The ‘Campus Drivers’ Finale May Have Revealed Who Season 2 Will Be About, and It’s a Change From the Books

Lois and Lane’s unfinished story could signal a major shift.

The season 1 finale of Campus Drivers left viewers with several questions, one of them being: Who will the protagonists of the next season be? Prime Video’s French-language series is based on the book series by C.S. Quill, but it has already demonstrated that it doesn’t necessarily intend to follow the novels’ path to the letter. And the very way Lane and Lois’s story ends could foreshadow an important narrative choice for the future of the adaptation.

As a reminder: In the series finale, Lane decides to leave for Los Angeles with his mother in order to allow Lois to keep her scholarship. Their story is cut short just when it seemed destined to turn into something more, leaving several questions open that could become central in a second season.

However, season 1 does also end with a twist involving Donovan. Without giving too much away about what happens in the second book, Crash Course, which is centered on Donovan, the ending sees (spoiler!) Donovan’s dad collapse during a basketball practice. Still, Lois and Lane remain at the center of the narrative with a story still entirely unresolved…and this choice may not be accidental.

The choice to keep Lane and Lois at the center of the story could suggest that Campus Drivers will not necessarily follow the narrative model of Off Campus. In the series based on Elle Kennedy’s novels, each season is designed to mainly tell the story of a new couple, giving space to different protagonists within the same narrative universe.

For Campus Drivers (despite the series having the same structure), the direction could be different: keeping Lane and Lois as central figures in the coming seasons as well, gradually developing the love stories of the other characters and giving them more and more space. Based on the season 1 ending, it’s possible that it will instead use an increasingly ensemble structure, in which the protagonists’ stories intertwine and become richer episode after episode, without necessarily making way for a new main couple each season.

This does not rule out the possibility that, further down the line, other characters could take on an even more important role. As early as the third season, for example, there could be room to explore the secondary love stories in greater depth. For now, however, these are only hypotheses; Prime Video has not yet confirmed what the structure of any future chapters will be.

If the series is renewed, it is reasonable to expect Lane and Lois to maintain a central role. The first season finale left their relationship hanging, and it would be difficult to imagine a follow-up that does not pick up precisely from what happened between them—especially because six episodes felt like too few to fully develop a dynamic between the leads.

The choice not to follow the structure of the novels could also be a way to differentiate Campus Drivers from Off Campus, especially since both series share the same platform and target the same audience of fans.

At the moment, a second season has not yet been officially confirmed. However, considering the attention the series is receiving and the narrative potential left open by the finale, it would not be surprising to see new updates arrive soon.

#6940. Just Turn the Heat On? That Might Be Why Your Skin Suddenly Feels So Dry

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

A few weeks ago, I rented a big cabin outside of the city with friends. It was the first cold weekend in New York, the kind where you might actually need to throw on the heavy jackets that were too much for those 55-degree fall days. Nestled in this mountain house, we cozied up around a fire in fleece sweatshirts and fuzzy blankets. Before bed, we turned the heat on. I slept cozy like a baby in the womb, but immediately upon waking, my skin felt tight and dry. I coated myself in moisturizer, but it was no use. My face—which is usually oily and acne-prone—was dull and rough, like it’d never produced a drop of oil in its 29 years.

At first, I thought it was a sign of the times. I’m about to turn 30, and my skin’s just getting drier. But when I headed back to Brooklyn, where my stuffy apartment produces enough natural heat on its own to not need the radiator cranked up, my skin went back to normal.

I’ve heard of this phenomenon before—when your skin gets dehydrated once you turn on the heat in your house—but this was my first experience. And I was determined to get to the bottom of it before my radiator goes on for good and the winter uglies take over. Two dermatologists weighed in—and it looks like this isn’t necessarily preventable but totally treatable.

“Indoor heating lowers the relative humidity in the air, which makes moisture evaporate from your skin more quickly,” says dermatologist Annette Czernik, MD. “Our skin naturally produces oils that help protect it and keep moisture in, but sometimes that's not enough when the air is really dry.” You might apply moisturizer and feel like you didn’t even do anything, she adds.

You’re also not always in the clear if you have oily skin, says Dr. Czernik. “A lot of people don't realize that your skin can be oily and dehydrated at the same time,” she explains. “This is because your skin can still produce oil while losing moisture from the dry air.”

You may also feel tightness, roughness, stinging, flaking, and even itching. “As your skin loses more moisture, its protective barrier can become weaker,” says Dr. Czernik, hence why you can experience some irritation as your skin isn’t as strong enough to fight it off. The air can also make your skin look dull or even feel more sensitive, since it’s working so hard to hold onto the moisture it needs, explains Dr. Czernik. “In more severe cases, you might experience redness, peeling, or even small cracks in your skin,” she adds.

Naturally, with colder air outside, your skin might get drier too, only exacerbating the issue. “When the temperature drops outside, the air usually holds less moisture,” says Dr. Czernik. “Then, when you go indoors, you're exposed to heated air that can also be very dry.” You’re basically losing moisture on both sides, no matter what you do. “Going back and forth between the cold outdoors and warm indoors can make that dryness more noticeable,” she adds. “This is why someone might have perfectly normal skin during the summer but struggle with dryness all winter.”

“When you turn the heat on, the air inside your home can become very dry,” explains dermatologist Ariel Ostad, MD. And one of the easiest ways to add moisture back into the air is a humidifier. “This can help slow down how quickly your skin loses water throughout the day or night,” Dr. Ostad adds.

He recommends getting a bedside humidifier, like the Canopy Bedside Humidifier, for your bedroom and running it while you’re sleeping. “You may notice that your skin feels less tight and dry when you wake up in the morning,” he says. “It can also help with dry lips and irritated skin.” But remember to clean it regularly, as bacteria and mold can build up in your device.

“If your skin starts getting drier, I would first look at the products you're using every day,” says Dr. Ostad. Both Dr. Ostad and Dr. Czernik recommend switching to a thicker moisturizer. Ideally, find one that both moisturizes (meaning it contains ingredients that pull water into your skin, like squalane, hyaluronic acid, panthenol, and glycerin) and seals in hydration (aka ingredients that act as a physical barrier to reduce moisture loss, including ceramides, oils, butters, and silicones). But you could also opt for two moisturizers nightly. I prefer to use a lightweight moisturizer, then follow it up with a barrier cream.

“It's also a good idea to apply your moisturizer right after washing your face or getting out of the shower,” adds Dr. Czernik. If your skin is still damp, even better. This helps pull all that water into your skin (which helps plump it up and decrease dullness), plus it eliminates time for moisture to evaporate from your skin.

Just as hot air can pull water from your skin, so can hot water, explains Dr. Czernik. It doesn’t mean you need to do a daily cold plunge, but opt for mild to warm water temperatures, as scalding water can also increase skin irritation.

Some of the products in your routine could actually be making your dryness worse. Put down all the strong exfoliants, whether it’s your salicylic acid cleanser, an acid-based peel pad, or a retinol serum.

Instead, Dr. Ostad suggests using a gentle, fragrance-free cleanser “that won't strip your skin of its natural oils.” And if you love your exfoliants, you don’t have to ditch them completely, he adds, but you might want to cut back to once or twice per week at most. “You don't need to completely change your skincare routine, but making a few small adjustments can really help during the colder months,” he says.

“If your skin is just a little dry, you can usually manage it at home with a good moisturizer,” says Dr. Ostad. “However, if the dryness continues for a few weeks despite changing your skincare routine, I would recommend seeing a dermatologist.” If you notice your skin becomes extremely itchy or irritated, that’s a good sign to see a doctor too. “Cracking, bleeding, or painful peeling are signs that something more serious could be going on,” adds Dr. Ostad.

Beth Gillette is the beauty editor at Cosmopolitan with eight years of experience researching, writing, and editing skin, hair, makeup, nail, and fragrance stories.

Beth Gillette is the beauty editor at Cosmopolitan, where she covers skincare, makeup, hair, nails, and more across digital and print. She can generally be found in bright eyeshadow furiously typing her latest feature or hemming and hawing about a new product you "have to try." Prior to Cosmopolitan, she wrote and edited beauty content as an Editor at The Everygirl for four years. Follow her on Instagram for makeup selfies and a new hair 'do every few months.

#6938. This 100-Year-Old Louisiana Home Got a New Plot of Land and a Colorful New Chapter

The expansive property also includes a pool house and carriage house.

Long before it became the backdrop for family dinners, afternoon swims, and spirited tennis matches, this century-old Colonial in Baton Rouge, Louisiana, had already lived a rather extraordinary life. In the late 1920s, the house was dismantled and relocated from Prairieville to its current address, where its distinctive red clay roof tiles—salvaged from Louisiana State University—have presided over a landscape of majestic live oaks ever since. Decades later, a young girl growing up down the street would admire the house, never imagining that one day she’d raise a family of her own beneath that very roof.

When she and her husband finally purchased the property, they knew that turning a beloved neighborhood landmark into a forever home for themselves and their three daughters would require a little imagination—and a lot of careful planning. Enter interior designer Anne Underwood, who was tasked with bringing the historic residence into the present while preserving the quirks that made it so beloved. Over several years, the project grew from a renovation of the original four-bedroom home into a sprawling family compound, complete with a carriage house, pool house, and landscaped grounds designed to make an ordinary weekend feel like a vacation.

Inside, Underwood embraced the home’s traditional bones without letting them stiffen the home (and the family’s) sunny disposition. Lemon-yellow checkerboard floors mingle with antique furnishings (an element Underwood says is a must for creating “a lived-in, collected-over-time feel that I find crucial to making a true home”), hand-painted finishes, and exuberant textiles. A whimsical chandelier welcomes guests into the living room, while a tucked-away banquette has become an unofficial headquarters for homework, puzzles, and art projects.

Outside, the property’s newest additions—including a pool house and carriage house—offer an equally playful proposition. Conceived as the estate’s crown jewel, the pool house features pale wood walls, a colorful handwoven light fixture, and a monumental wood-burning pizza oven. The homeowners’ relatives jokingly dubbed the expanding property their own private resort, a description Underwood happily embraces.

“So much was poured into this project to make it unique, joyful, and really functional for the clients,” says Underwood. “The warmth and care of each detailed layer creates a sense of ease, which is what facilitates the joyfulness that plays out inside and outside these walls.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Anne UnderwoodArchitect: LRK ArchitectsContractor: Bernhardt Normand ConstructionStyling: Lucy BammanLandscape Architect: CARBOLocation: Baton Rouge, LouisianaThe Space: A four-bedroom, three-and-a-half-bathroom main house, with a carriage house and pool house.

With a simpler architectural structure, the pool house was a place where Underwood leaned into the family’s playful energy. “We selected the wall stain to look like the inside of a shell and wanted the furnishings to feel layered, colorful, vibrant—like a vacation but also very durable for wet swimsuits and sweaty tennis players!” she adds.

The first selection for the pool house was also its most exuberant: a 12-light PET Lamp fixture, with each colorful pendant and cord individually customized and positioned beneath the soaring vaulted ceiling. “Its colorful, playful nature was exactly what we wanted and is very effective for balancing light throughout the space with high vaulted ceilings,” says Underwood. Below, a generous sectional upholstered in indoor-outdoor fabric creates an inviting place to retreat after a swim.

With vibrant Moroccan mosaic flooring, blue zellige shower tile, and a vintage wooden mirror, the pool house bathroom could easily belong to a boutique hotel on some far-flung island. Wood siding and thoughtfully chosen antique details lend the space a relaxed, resort-inspired character that feels right at home beside the pool.

Tradition gets a playful twist in the home’s primary gathering space, where a hand-knotted rug and classic upholstered furnishings mingle with an exuberant chandelier by Stray Dog Designs.

“This house lacks a foyer, so we wanted the living room to be welcoming with plenty of seating and layers of color and accessories,” says Underwood. “While this is the more ‘formal’ living space, we selected a bright, whimsical light fixture to let guests know that these homeowners don’t take themselves (or their interiors) too seriously.”

A passageway between the living room and the home’s more casual quarters, the dining room needed to make an impression without feeling overly precious. Schumacher’s Weeping Pine wallpaper wraps the space in pattern, while pale blue trim, upholstered chairs, and vintage sconces sourced from Round Top lend the formal setting an unexpected sense of ease.

With the adjoining breakfast room to consider, Underwood gave the kitchen a quieter disposition. Existing cabinetry received a custom pale green stain, expertly applied by a local faux-finish artist, while paneled appliances and tonal counter stools create a seamless effect. The yellow-and-white checkerboard flooring continues underfoot, connecting the two spaces.

A powder room is as good a place as any to indulge a dramatic streak. “We wanted a bold escape for guests in the powder bath, and this incredible grasscloth wallpaper was the jumping-off point,” says Underwood. She complemented the Aux Abris pattern’s lush, atmospheric tone with a wood-grained antique chest repurposed as a marble-topped vanity. Vintage Murano glass sconces and a striped inlay mirror add a final dose of eccentricity.

In the office, Underwood leaned into the appeal of a well-appointed study, furnishing this hardworking space almost entirely with vintage and antique finds. Custom cabinetry painted in Benjamin Moore’s Templeton Gray conceals printers, files, and other less-than-picturesque necessities, while mohair-upholstered armchairs make the space equally suited to work and contemplation.

As it turns out, the inspiration for the primary suite was waiting just outside the window. “I started with the idea of bringing materials—like the patinated copper on all exterior lanterns and gutters—from the exterior to the interior,” says Underwood. “While the walls are painted a green/blue color reminiscent of the patina of weathered copper, the accents in the room are warm shades of camel and copper.”

A Gustavian antique secretary and dimensional papier-maché art from Gusto complete the room's quietly collected atmosphere.

The generously proportioned soaking tub in the primary bathroom has already lived several lives, first as a favorite bathing spot for the family’s three young daughters, and now as a welcome retreat for their parents. Underwood carried the bedroom’s blue-green palette into the bath, introducing blush-patterned wallpaper, graphic stone tile, and black accents to balance the room’s softer flourishes.

In daughter Emily’s room, Underwood chose to embrace the sloped ceiling, outfitting it with custom bed canopies that create an enveloping, almost storybook atmosphere. Layers of patterned bedding, including bespoke eiderdowns made in the UK, heighten the cozy effect, while chunky upholstered ottomans offer a welcome counterpoint to the room’s more feminine details.

Making sense of the home’s quirky upstairs architecture required some clever spatial maneuvering. Underwood divided one large bathroom into two separate spaces, each with its own dressing vanity. In daughter Olivia’s bath, Salvesen Graham floral wallpaper covers the sloped walls and ceiling, visually softening the room’s unusual angles. “The pattern play in the tile, wallpaper, and fabrics keeps the energy moving throughout the space,” she adds.

In the carriage house, Underwood continued the layered approach with velvet, chenille, and a generously proportioned sectional, creating a retreat where overnight guests can comfortably settle in for an afternoon—or an entire weekend.

The carriage house kitchen offers guests all the comforts of home, with a nostalgic twist. Cabinetry painted Benjamin Moore’s Mistletoe features vintage-inspired vent detailing, while a retro SMEG refrigerator reinforces the room’s charming, slightly old-fashioned character.

Underwood preserved the home’s original red clay roof tiles while refreshing its facade using Benjamin Moore’s White Dove on the siding and Kennebunkport Green on the shutters. An expanded bluestone sidewalk gives the front door prominence while mature live oaks lend the property a sense of permanence that no renovation could replicate.

Anne Underwood is the founder of Underwood Interiors. Known for her thoughtful approach to creating layered, Anne creates livable spaces that balance timeless tradition with a fresh, playful sensibility. With an appreciation for antiques, vintage furnishings, and artisanal craftsmanship, her interiors feel collected over time rather than decorated all at once. Her work celebrates color, pattern, and architectural character, with an emphasis on thoughtful details and everyday functionality. Whether reimagining a historic residence or designing a new space from the ground up, she believes the most successful interiors reflect the personalities of the people who call them home.

#6937. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

Microsoft has announced that it's redesigning Copilot, the company's all-encompassing AI assistant, so users can hand off complex tasks, build tools by describing what they need and assign work to an agent that keeps going after they log off.

#6934. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

#6930. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

#6929. Before & After: How an Artist and Architect Couple Turned the “Worst House” on the Block Into Their Dream Home

In Brooklyn, artist Katherine Keltner and architect Douglas Gauthier transform a crumbling townhouse with equal parts drama and restraint.

Before they stepped foot inside the crumbling brownstone on a landmark block in Brooklyn’s Boerum Hill, artist Katherine Keltner and architect Douglas Gauthier had toured 30 homes and browsed more than 200 online. Neither of them expected to become instantly captivated by the 1869 Italianate townhouse, which was being sold in a stately yet dilapidated condition, having once served as a single-room occupancy (SRO) residence. But it happened nonetheless.

“This was the first one that had the bones that Katherine wanted,” recalls Gauthier, who runs the firm GAuthier Architects. “It follows the classic real estate rule of buying the worst house on the best block.”

The strategy paid off: Once their offer was accepted, the couple embarked on an 18-month-long odyssey that saw them jump through a series of hoops (preservationists; concerned neighbors; skeptical engineers) to completely transform the moldering pile into a creative haven for them to live, work, and raise their daughter.

A true joint-effort, Gauthier oversaw a modest two-story rear expansion and structural alterations, while Keltner took charge of spatial arrangements as well as color, pattern, texture, and artwork—much of it her own.

Once deteriorating, the interiors now have an easy elegance, with plaster walls, arched passageways, and curved corners that give the soaring ceilings an intimate feel. Pink takes an unexpected starring role, appearing throughout the parlor floor and also in the zellige-clad primary bath, but other adventurous hues like mint green and crimson also feature prominently. Capping it off is a plywood-sheathed office for Gauthier’s practice, allowing the family retreat to double as a place of creative inspiration.

Below, Keltner and Gauthier share their biggest lessons (and some “before” pictures) from the painstaking passion project.

When the townhouse was converted to an SRO, it had been chopped up into at least 13 units and two kitchens, and few of the original decorative elements were still intact. A couple had bought it in the 1960s and made it a family home again and added an art gallery space. But by 2021, when Keltner and Gauthier first saw it, the house was in a state of severe neglect, with peeling paint, crumbling floors, ancient appliances—not to mention a 1,500-pound printing press in the studio (that the couple later preserved). Yet, Keltner and Gauthier saw an opportunity, not to recreate the past, but rather forge their own set of rules.

“The house wasn’t burdened with pristine historic details—our moldings were trashed,” says Gauthier. “There were a few things that we kept and celebrated, but to me, it was like a tabula rasa. We can just go in and make it ours.” Katherine agreed: “I feel like sometimes having to preserve things is more expensive than just starting clean,” she says. “And so I thought that was an advantage for us.”

Not having to be too precious with ornamentation allowed the couple to make a bold statement about crown moldings, eschewing tradition and installing curving corners and coved ceilings instead. Nowhere does the effect have more impact than in the parlor, where Gauthier devised a ceiling light in the shape of a giant oval that casts light upward rather than down. “I really like indirect lighting,” he says.

Crafted out of laser-cut plywood, the elliptical fixture is an abstracted homage to the ceiling medallions found in neighboring historic brownstones. “Some of them are incredibly ornate and beautiful, but they don't really have any use—they’re just decoration,” says Gauthier. “Adding ambient lighting to the living space takes that historical reference in pocket.”

Once they were through the process of changing the structure’s zoning from an SRO to a single-family home, Gauthier and Keltner had to contend with its landmarked status, meaning a series of approvals were required for any additions or exterior modifications.

The couple originally planned to construct a three-story extension in the back, but they ended up compromising on two floors. This expansion includes a bump-out for the parlor floor’s kitchen, leading to a rear deck that steps down to the garden. On top, a private terrace connects to the primary bath. “Instead of having that third story, we now have a deck off of our primary suite,” says Keltner. “It’s kind of amazing,” adds Gauthier.

One decision the couple wrestled with mightily was whether to replace the original, 150-year-old pine flooring with ebony-stained white oak. “The floors were part of the reason we liked the house so much,” says Gauthier. But their contractor talked them out of keeping them. “It’s a lot of extra work for them,” says Keltner. “They have to take it up, save it, put something else down, and then put it back. I think we reached a point in time and money where we were like, Okay, fine.”

Another flooring regret was not adding underfoot heating. “I was pushing the mechanical engineers to do radiant heating and radiant cooling,” says Gauthier. “Radiant heat is really lovely—you have warmth under your feet all the time.” But the mechanics behind the system proved complicated and technically redundant, as the home was already configured with a forced-air HVAC system.

“In doing research and other projects, it’s clear that the cost-to-pleasure ratio would have been worth it,” he says now. “We regret not putting the warm floors in.”

While windows might sometimes be an afterthought, Keltner and Gauthier have advice: Go with triple-glazed. “Windows provide heat and cool insulation as well as security,” explains Keltner. “And they can define both the outside and the inside of a space.” The couple went with triple-glazed windows from Austrian-based firm Bewiso that come pre-wired for easy connection to alarm systems. “They’re almost like hurricane windows,” she says.

“You can't break through three panes of glass,” says Gauthier. Due to the building’s landmark status, they had to use historic-style windows on the front facade. “We were able to get these old-fashioned type of windows that when you close them, they feel like you're closing a Mercedes,” says Gauthier. “They completely silence the outdoors.”

The final piece of advice is perhaps the most crucial: “Hire the best contractor you can afford,” says Gauthier.

“The typical advice is to get a few bids and throw out the lowest and the highest,” says Keltner. “People usually take the second lowest.” However, that could be the determining factor between an on-time project and one that drags. “You’ll end up paying the difference both in money and time,” she says. “It’s often hard for people to bite the bullet and go with the slightly higher price, but it really pays off.”

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

He made a stop at Jake Shane’s podcast, Therapuss, during his press tour on November 11 and made rare comments about his wife—yes, wife—Bryana Holly.

I know what you’re thinking: How long has it been since Nicholas Hoult and Jennifer Lawrence called it quits, and when did he start dating Bryana? Ahead, find everything we know about Nicholas Hoult’s wife, Bryana Holly.

#6922. Report: Cyber Criminals Move Toward More Targeted Phishing Attacks

Cyber criminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, increasingly using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4. The security firm found that spear-phishing attacks increased 41% over the past year.

The company's Phishing Threat Trends Report, Vol. 8 found that overall phishing volume increased 29.8% year over year. Organizations received an average of one dangerous e-mail for every 733 messages, but the bigger concern is how attackers are changing their approach.

Rather than sending the same fraudulent message to thousands of people, criminals are spending more time researching their targets and creating convincing messages that appear to come from trusted coworkers, businesses, and technology platforms.

The findings are based on threat data from KnowBe4 Defend, the company's inbound e-mail security platform, which processes approximately 1.15 billion event signals daily.

KnowBe4 found that several traditional phishing methods declined significantly compared with 2025. Mobile-focused scams dropped 47.3%, advance-fee fraud fell 31.3%, and missed-message scams declined 11.3%.

Meanwhile, spear phishing increased 41.1%, pre-attack reconnaissance rose 33.7%, and company impersonation climbed 28%.

The shift suggests that attackers are finding more value in carefully crafted scams that exploit existing business relationships and workplace routines.

Business e-mail compromise (BEC) remains particularly common, accounting for 51.2% of phishing e-mails analyzed in the report. Human resources, finance departments, and executive leadership are among the most frequently targeted groups.

Attackers also appear to be timing their campaigns around normal business activity. KnowBe4 found that 90.4% of phishing messages arrive Monday through Friday, with Monday accounting for the largest share at 20.6%.

One of the report's more troubling findings involves the growing use of legitimate technology platforms to deliver malicious content.

KnowBe4 found that phishing attacks were twice as likely to originate from legitimate infrastructure in the third quarter of 2026 compared with the previous quarter.

That includes AI services, where attackers are taking advantage of trusted domains to host fake developer consoles, login prompts, and credential-stealing pages. The report also identified a 228% increase in AI-brand impersonation attacks between March and August.

Because these malicious pages can operate on legitimate domains with valid security certificates, users may not see the usual warning signs associated with suspicious websites.

"For years, security advice has focused on a predictable checklist: Check the sender address, hover over the link, and look for the padlock," said Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence at KnowBe4.

"While still foundational, that guidance is becoming less useful by the day."

The findings echo a recent report about management software and AI e-mail assistants. Research from Fortra and Barracuda showed how criminals are using legitimate administrative tools and hidden AI instructions to manipulate users and compromise systems.

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time.

#6918. What Does the Winner of ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Get, Exactly?

It’s a very different situation for the pro vs. the celeb.

Dancing With the Stars is wrapping up season 35 and someone is about to walk away with a glittery mirrorball trophy and a bunch of money. Actually several someones are going to walk away with a bunch of money, because Dancing With the Stars shares the love between multiple contestants when it comes to winnings. If you have questions, we have answers, so let’s get to it.

Nope. The winner of Dancing With the Stars doesn’t get extra money for winning—they just get the Mirrorball Trophy. However, they do get financially compensated for their time on the show—within an established payment structure.

The cast makes money for each week they remain on the show. According to Variety, everyone takes home $125,000 for the rehearsal period, as well as the first two weeks DWTS airs—meaning, even if you’re voted off you still get paid. The remaining celebs then get bonuses each week they continue.

A while back, there were reports (via Variety) that contestants got an additional $10,000 for weeks three and four, an additional $20,000 for weeks five and six, an additional $30,000 for weeks seven and eight, and then another $50,000 for weeks nine and ten.

This means that all the final celebs who make it to the Dancing With the Stars finale earn $295,000, regardless of whether or not they win.

Pros earn a per-episode salary. We have a full breakdown this week, but according to Us Weekly the highest-paid dancers (aka the pros who have been around for the longest) make around $5,000 an episode.

But there’s no extra baked-in “prize” for the pros if they secure a win, though there is a bonus. Jenna Johnson explained how it works to The Morning After podcast, saying “Whoever makes it to the finale, you all get paid up until then. And you do get a nice bonus. If you win, it’s not like you get a million dollars. You’re just getting a cute trophy together.”

Jenna also confirmed that pros who are voted out earlier are “guaranteed until a certain amount of weeks” pay. Which is something Brandon Armstrong’s wife, Brylee, confirmed on TikTok last year, saying “Brandon is still very much a pro on the show. Just because his partner was eliminated doesn’t mean that he just gets the boot and he’s gotta pack his bags and go all the way back to Utah.” Brylee added that “Dancing with the Stars takes care of their people, so no need to worry.”

#6917. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.
